Britannica Editors Last updated Sep. 2, 2026 •History Britannica AI Ask Anything Table of Contents Table of Contents Ask Anything Because influenza epidemics and pandemics can devastate large regions of the world very quickly, WHO constantly monitors influenza disease activity on a global scale. This monitoring is useful for gathering information that can be used to prepare vaccines and that can be disseminated to health centres in countries where seasonal influenza outbreaks are likely to occur. Monitoring by WHO also plays an important role in preventing and preparing for potential epidemics and pandemics. In the event that a potentially pandemic influenza virus emerges, WHO adheres to its influenza pandemic preparedness plan. This plan consists of six phases of pandemic alert. Phases 1–3, which are the early stages in pandemic preparedness, are designed to prevent or contain small outbreaks. In these early phases, isolated incidences of animal-to-human transmission of an influenza virus are observed and provide warning that a virus has pandemic potential. Later, small outbreaks of disease may occur, generally resulting from multiple cases of animal-to-human transmission. Phase 3 signals to affected countries that the implementation of efforts to control the outbreak is needed to prevent a pandemic. Phases 4 and 5 are characterized by increasing urgency in mitigating the outbreak. Confirmed human-to-human viral transmission, with sustained disease in human communities which subsequently spread so that disease transmission between humans occurred in two countries, indicates that a pandemic is imminent. Phase 6, the highest level of pandemic alert, is characterized by widespread disease and sustained transmission of the virus between humans. Influenza pandemics sometimes occur in waves. Thus, a post-pandemic phase, when disease activity decreases, may be followed by another period of high prevalence of disease. As a result, influenza pandemics may last for a period of months (see pandemic). Transmission and symptoms The flu may affect individuals of all ages, though the highest incidence of the disease is among children and young adults. Influenza is generally more frequent during the colder months of the year. Infection is transmitted from person to person through the respiratory tract, by such means as inhalation of infected droplets resulting from coughing and sneezing. As the virus particles gain entrance to the body, they selectively attack and destroy the ciliated epithelial cells that line the upper respiratory tract, bronchial tubes, and trachea. The incubation period of the disease is one to two days, after which the onset of symptoms is abrupt, with sudden and distinct chills, fatigue, and muscle aches. The temperature rises rapidly to 38–40 °C (101–104 °F). A diffuse headache and severe muscular aches throughout the body are experienced, often accompanied by irritation or a sense of rawness in the throat. In three to four days the temperature begins to fall, and the person begins to recover. Symptoms associated with respiratory tract infection, such as coughing and nasal discharge, become more prominent and may be accompanied by lingering feelings of weakness. Death may occur, usually among older people already weakened by other debilitating disorders, and is caused in most of those cases by complications such as pneumonia or bronchitis. Treatment and prevention Standard treatment for influenza is bed rest, ingestion of fluids, and the use of analgesics to control fever. It is recommended that children and adolescents with the flu not be given aspirin, as treatment of viral infections with aspirin is associated with Reye syndrome, a very serious illness. The antiviral drugs amantadine and rimantadine have beneficial effects on cases of influenza involving the type A virus. However, viral resistance to these agents has reduced their effectiveness. The neuraminidase inhibitors oseltamivir (Tamiflu) and zanamivir (Relenza), introduced in the late 1990s, inhibit both the influenza A and B viruses. These drugs can shorten the duration and severity of symptoms and may be used to prevent infection following exposure. Baloxavir marboxil (Xofluza), introduced in 2018, is a cap-dependent endonuclease (CEN) inhibitor; CEN serves an essential role in enabling influenza viruses to hijack host cell machinery for viral replication. By blocking this critical step in viral replication, the drug rapidly decreases the amount of virus in the respiratory tract, helping to quickly reduce symptoms. It is effective against both influenza A and B. Why don't flu shots always work?Learn about the search for a universal flu vaccine.(more) See all videos for this article Individual protection against the flu may be bolstered by injection of a vaccine containing two or more circulating influenza viruses. These viruses are produced in chick embryos and rendered noninfective; standard commercial preparations ordinarily include the type B influenza virus and several of the A subtypes. Protection from one vaccination seldom lasts more than a year, and yearly vaccination may be recommended, particularly for those individuals who are unusually susceptible to influenza or whose weak condition could lead to serious complications in case of infection. However, routine immunization in healthy people is also recommended. Advances in scientific understanding of influenza and vaccine technologies introduced the prospect of developing a so-called universal influenza vaccine, capable of protecting individuals against a broad range of different influenza subtypes; several candidates are in various stages of clinical trials. In order to prevent human-infecting bird flu viruses from mutating into more dangerous subtypes, public health authorities try to limit the viral “reservoir” where antigenic shift may take place by ordering the destruction of infected poultry flocks. The Editors of Encyclopaedia Britannica This article was most recently revised and updated by Kara Rogers. Britannica Editors Last updated July 30, 2026 •History Britannica AI Ask Anything Quick Summary Table of Contents Table of Contents Quick Summary Ask Anything Top Questions What is the human respiratory system? The human respiratory system facilitates the continuous exchange of oxygen and carbon dioxide between the body and the environment. This vital process involves two main mechanisms: convection and diffusion. The system consists of the upper and lower airway systems. The upper airway includes the nose, paranasal cavities, pharynx, and oral cavity. The lower airway comprises the larynx, trachea, stem bronchi, intrapulmonary bronchi, bronchioles, and alveolar ducts. The diaphragm, the main respiratory muscle, along with the intercostal muscles, facilitates the pumping action of the lungs. Blood and the circulatory system are essential for gas transport. 3 Britannica Sources childhood disease and disorder: Respiratory disorders human respiratory system: The intrapulmonary conducting airways: bronchi and bronchioles diaphragm This answer is created from Britannica articles using AI. AI can make mistakes, so verify using Britannica articles. How does the human body take in oxygen and remove carbon dioxide? The human respiratory system facilitates the exchange of oxygen and carbon dioxide through ventilation and diffusion. Air enters the lungs, where oxygen diffuses from the alveoli into the blood and binds to hemoglobin in red blood cells. Simultaneously, carbon dioxide moves from the blood into the alveoli to be exhaled. This diffusion is driven by differences in partial pressures: oxygen moves from the air into the blood, while carbon dioxide moves from the blood into the air. The blood transports these gases between the lungs and the body's tissues. 2 Britannica Sources 13 Questions About How the Human Body Works Answered: How do people breathe in and out? human respiratory system: Gas exchange in the lung This answer is created from Britannica articles using AI. AI can make mistakes, so verify using Britannica articles. What are the main organs involved in breathing? The primary organs involved in breathing work together to supply the body with oxygen and expel carbon dioxide. Lungs The lungs are the main organs for gas exchange. Within the lungs, oxygen and carbon dioxide move between the air and the blood across the respiratory exchange surfaces. Diaphragm This dome-shaped muscle located below the lungs is essential for breathing. When it contracts, the diaphragm increases the height of the thoracic cavity, which lowers the pressure inside and causes air to flow into the lungs. Trachea The trachea, or windpipe, is a tube that carries air from the larynx to the lungs. Bronchi The trachea divides into two main bronchi that enter the lungs. These bronchi further divide into smaller and narrower airways called bronchioles, which distribute air to the gas-exchanging zone of the lung. 6 Britannica Sources pulmonary alveolus trachea bronchus human skeleton: The rib cage human respiratory system: The trachea and the stem bronchi diaphragm This answer is created from Britannica articles using AI. AI can make mistakes, so verify using Britannica articles. What is the process of gas exchange in the lungs? In the lungs, gas exchange occurs across respiratory exchange surfaces between air and blood. The structure of the human lung provides a large internal surface area with a thin membrane that enhances gas exchange between the alveoli and pulmonary capillaries. The process of diffusion drives gas exchange, moving gases from areas of higher partial pressure to lower partial pressure. Oxygen and carbon dioxide are transported between tissue cells and the lungs via blood. The efficiency of gas exchange relies on the uniform distribution of blood flow and inspired air throughout the lungs. 1 Britannica Source human respiratory system: Gas exchange in the lung This answer is created from Britannica articles using AI. AI can make mistakes, so verify using Britannica articles. How do the diaphragm and intercostal muscles help with breathing? The diaphragm and intercostal muscles facilitate breathing through mechanical actions that change the volume of the chest cavity. The diaphragm, a dome-shaped muscle separating the chest and abdominal cavities, contracts and moves downward to increase the chest cavity's height, which lowers the internal pressure and draws air into the lungs. The external intercostal muscles contract, lifting the ribs up and outward to expand the chest circumference. During expiration, these muscles relax, and the chest wall and lungs' elastic recoil force air out. The internal intercostal muscles depress the rib cage to decrease chest circumference during labored breathing or exercise. 3 Britannica Sources respiratory system: Mammals human skeleton: The rib cage diaphragm This answer is created from Britannica articles using AI. AI can make mistakes, so verify using Britannica articles. What controls the rate and depth of breathing in the body? Breathing rate and depth are automatically controlled by neuron networks in the hindbrain, specifically the pons and medulla. These networks manage the muscles in the thorax and abdomen, creating pressure gradients for air movement in and out of the lungs. The respiratory system adjusts breathing patterns based on internal and external changes via sensors throughout the body. Chemoreceptors identify changes in blood oxygen levels and acidity, while mechanoreceptors monitor lung expansion and muscle contraction. The rate and depth of breathing increase with the activity of chemoreceptors caused by hypoxia or increased carbon dioxide, which helps to restore usual oxygen and carbon dioxide levels. 3 Britannica Sources medulla oblongata blood: Respiration human respiratory system: Central chemoreceptors This answer is created from Britannica articles using AI. The design of the respiratory system 1 of 2 Passage of air through the respiratory tract explainedThe respiratory tract conveys air from the mouth and nose to the lungs, where oxygen and carbon dioxide are exchanged between the alveoli and the capillaries.(more) See all videos for this article 2 of 2 human nasal cavitySagittal view of the human nasal cavity.(more) The human gas-exchanging organ, the lung, is located in the thorax, where its delicate tissues are protected by the bony and muscular thoracic cage. The lung provides the tissues of the human body with a continuous flow of oxygen and clears the blood of the gaseous waste product, carbon dioxide. Atmospheric air is pumped in and out regularly through a system of pipes, called conducting airways, which join the gas-exchange region with the outside of the body. The airways can be divided into upper and lower airway systems. The transition between the two systems is located where the pathways of the respiratory and digestive systems cross, just at the top of the larynx. The upper airway system comprises the nose and the paranasal cavities (or sinuses), the pharynx (or throat), and partly also the oral cavity, since it may be used for breathing. The lower airway system consists of the larynx, the trachea, the stem bronchi, and all the airways ramifying intensively within the lungs, such as the intrapulmonary bronchi, the bronchioles, and the alveolar ducts. For respiration, the collaboration of other organ systems is clearly essential. The diaphragm, as the main respiratory muscle, and the intercostal muscles of the chest wall play an essential role by generating, under the control of the central nervous system, the pumping action on the lung. The muscles expand and contract the internal space of the thorax, the bony framework of which is formed by the ribs and the thoracic vertebrae. The contribution of the lung and chest wall (ribs and muscles) to respiration is described below in The mechanics of breathing. 葛兰素史克的mRNA季节性流感疫苗进入III期临床试验 - 全球生物防御

GSK’s mRNA Flu Vaccine Heads to Phase III - Global Biodefense
新闻简报GSK has advanced its investigational mRNA seasonal flu vaccine candidate to Phase III trials following positive Phase II results presented at the OPTIONS XIII Conference in Washington, D.C., on September 1, 2026. The vaccine, designated FLUm3HA.b-3NA, targets both hemagglutinin (HA) and neuraminidase (NA) proteins of the flu virus, a dual-antigen approach not yet tested in Phase III trials globally. The trial is set to begin this month and will assess immunogenicity and safety data through day 181 post-vaccination. GSK’s vaccine showed higher immune responses against all influenza strains tested compared to standard-dose and high-dose inactivated flu vaccines in clinical trials. The FDA granted GSK’s candidate Fast Track designation in July 2026 for its targeted age indication. Moderna’s mFlusiva, the first mRNA influenza vaccine to reach the market, demonstrated approximately 26.6 percent greater effectiveness in preventing laboratory-confirmed influenza compared to traditional vaccines in clinical trials. GSK’s entry into Phase III positions it as a second-mover in the mRNA flu space, but with a differentiated scientific rationale. The underlying numerical data has not been released publicly, and results were shared in an oral presentation at the conference. The development comes in a competitive landscape where seasonal influenza causes one billion infections annually, with conventional vaccines showing variable effectiveness ranging from 20 to 60 percent over the past 15 years.
查看新闻五要素
时间
葛兰素史克于2026年9月1日宣布,将在OPTIONS XIII流感控制会议中展示积极的II期试验结果后,将其研究性mRNA季节性流感疫苗候选疫苗推进至关键的III期临床试验。该试验计划于本月开始。
地点与对象
该发展涉及季节性流感病毒,包括人类流感甲型H1N1流感病毒pdm09、甲型H3N2流感病毒以及B/Victoria和B/Yamagata谱系,这些是该疫苗在目标群体中的作用对象。
事件
葛兰素史克的mRNA季节性流感疫苗候选疫苗在积极的II期试验结果后进入III期临床试验,采用双抗原策略,同时针对流感病毒的血凝素(HA)和神经氨酸酶(NA)蛋白。
规模、影响与风险
The trial assessed immunogenicity and safety data through day 181 post-vaccination, with results showing higher immune responses against all influenza strains tested compared to licensed standard-dose and high-dose inactivated flu vaccines. Moderna’s mFlusiva demonstrated approximately 26.6 percent greater effectiveness at preventing laboratory-confirmed influenza compared to traditional vaccines in clinical trials.
应对、状态与不确定性
葛兰素史克尚未公开发布相关数值数据;详细结果是在OPTIONS XIII会议上通过口头报告分享的。美国食品药品监督管理局(FDA)于2026年7月授予了GSK候选疫苗针对特定年龄群体的快速通道(Fast Track)资格。

GSK’s mRNA Flu Vaccine Heads to Phase III - Global Biodefense

Original source text (English)
Close Menu Facebook X (Twitter) Instagram Facebook Instagram Threads Subscribe Featured Ebola Outbreak Avian Influenza Measles Resurgence Vaccine News Events Advertisement Medical Countermeasures GSK’s mRNA Flu Vaccine Heads to Phase III Dual-antigen approach could raise the bar in a market already transformed by Moderna's recent mRNA approval By Global Biodefense StaffSeptember 2, 2026 Flu virus particles. Credit: NIAID Share Facebook LinkedIn Reddit Email A new generation of influenza vaccines may be taking shape. GSK announced on Sept. 1, 2026, that it will advance its investigational mRNA seasonal flu vaccine candidate into a pivotal Phase III trial following positive Phase II results presented at the OPTIONS XIII Conference for the Control of Influenza in Washington, D.C. The trial is set to begin this month. The development arrives just weeks after Moderna secured U.S. Food and Drug Administration approval for mFlusiva, the first mRNA influenza vaccine to reach market, approved on Aug. 5 for adults 50 and older. GSK’s candidate is pursuing a different and potentially broader strategy, one that has not yet been tested in a Phase III trial anywhere in the world. A Two-Target Strategy Most licensed flu vaccines, including Moderna’s newly approved mFlusiva, focus on hemagglutinin (HA), the protein that allows the flu virus to attach to and enter human cells. GSK’s candidate, designated FLUm3HA.b-3NA, is designed to also target neuraminidase (NA), the protein responsible for helping newly replicated virus particles spread from infected cells to healthy ones. Growing evidence suggests that engaging NA alongside HA could improve protection, reduce illness severity, and limit transmission. No approved flu vaccine currently takes this dual approach. In the Flu-028 Phase II trial, a randomized, observer-blind study enrolling 971 adults aged 18 and older, GSK’s vaccine candidate generated higher immune responses against all influenza strains tested compared to licensed standard-dose and high-dose inactivated flu vaccines, in younger and older adult groups respectively. The trial assessed immunogenicity and safety data through day 181 post-vaccination. GSK has not yet released the underlying numerical data publicly; detailed results were shared in an oral presentation at the OPTIONS XIII conference. The FDA granted GSK’s candidate Fast Track designation in July 2026 for its targeted age indication, a regulatory tool used to expedite development of therapies addressing unmet medical needs. An Evolving and Competitive Landscape Seasonal influenza causes approximately one billion infections annually, including up to five million severe cases and up to 650,000 respiratory deaths worldwide, according to the World Health Organization. Conventional flu vaccines have shown variable effectiveness ranging from roughly 20 to 60 percent over the past 15 years, leaving substantial room for improvement. Moderna’s mFlusiva demonstrated approximately 26.6 percent greater effectiveness at preventing laboratory-confirmed influenza compared to traditional vaccines in clinical trials, with a stronger immune response in adults 65 and older. The vaccine has been tested in roughly 70,000 clinical trial participants and is now entering its first real-world flu season, with questions about pricing, availability, and uptake still unresolved. GSK’s entry into Phase III positions it as a second-mover in the mRNA flu space, but with a differentiated scientific rationale. Moderna had previously explored HA and NA dual-targeting in its own pipeline but paused that work amid broader pipeline reprioritization. GSK is now carrying that hypothesis forward into late-stage testing. FDA更新本季流感疫苗;Bronson医生呼吁提前流感准备 - wwmtarc

FDA updates this season's flu shot; Bronson doctor urges early flu prep - wwmtarc
新闻简报The article discusses the FDA's update to the 2026-2027 flu vaccine, which includes a new H3N2 strain that emerged after the previous season's vaccine was formulated. A Bronson doctor, William Workman, highlights the potential for this strain to remain dominant and urges early preparation for the upcoming flu season. The 2025-2026 flu season was particularly severe, with an estimated 26 million illnesses, 340,000 hospitalizations, and 21,000 deaths in the U.S., driven largely by the H3N2 variant. The CDC reports that 88% of subtyped influenza A-positive specimens during that season were H3N2, indicating its significant role. Workman emphasizes that while the vaccine may not guarantee complete protection against infection, it aims to reduce the risk of severe illness and complications, especially for vulnerable groups such as those with chronic medical conditions, young children, and older adults. The article also notes that influenza can spread quickly through households, with an incubation period of one to two days.

时间
2025-2026流感季,2026-2027流感季即将来临。文章于2026年9月2日发表。
地点与对象
密歇根州卡拉马祖市和美国。人群包括患有慢性病的个体、儿童和老年人。
事件
FDA更新了2026-2027季流感疫苗,以包含在上一季疫苗制备后新出现的H3N2毒株。Bronson医生威廉·沃克曼强调了由于H3N2变异株可能占据主导地位,提前为流感季做准备的重要性。
规模、影响与风险
The 2025-2026 flu season caused an estimated 26 million illnesses, 340,000 hospitalizations, and 21,000 deaths in the U.S. The H3N2 variant was the dominant strain, with 88% of subtyped influenza A-positive specimens being H3N2. The new vaccine includes this strain to reduce the risk of severe illness and hospitalizations.
应对、状态与不确定性
FDA根据抗原漂移和流行中流感病毒的分析更新了疫苗。CDC和Bronson医生建议人们为流感季做好准备,尽管疫苗在防止感染方面存在局限性,但仍强调其在降低疾病严重程度方面的重要性。

A Bronson doctor, William Workman, highlights the potential for this strain to remain dominant and urges early preparation for the upcoming flu season. The 2025-2026 flu season was particularly severe, with an estimated 26 million illnesses, 340,000 hospitalizations, and 21,000 deaths in the U.S., driven largely by the H3N2 variant. The CDC reports that 88% of subtyped influenza A-positive specimens during that season were H3N2, indicating its significant role. Workman emphasizes that while the vaccine may not guarantee complete protection against infection, it aims to reduce the risk of severe illness and complications, especially for vulnerable groups such as those with chronic medical conditions, young children, and older adults. The article also notes that influenza can spread quickly through households, with an incubation period of one to two days.

(Photo illustration by Joe Raedle/Getty Images) Share topics: Flu vaccine H3N2 FDA Influenza A Vaccine update CDC Vaccination rates Seasonal influenza KALAMAZOO, Mich. — After a particularly severe flu season that sickened millions of Americans and sent hundreds of thousands to the hospital, a Bronson physician is urging people to prepare now as the 2026-27 flu season approaches. Dr. William Workman, an internal medicine physician with Bronson and chief of primary care, says a mutated strain of influenza A (H3N2) played a major role in last season’s surge. He believes it could remain the dominant strain this season. “We saw a variant strain come through the hospitals, it was called H3N2 variant. This was kind of a new strain that had evolved recently that caused the increase in office visits and hospitalizations,” Workman said. Weather: Strong to severe storms may bring damaging winds this afternoon and evening The 2025-26 flu season brought significant illness across the United States. Powered By 10 Bronco nation still reeling from UofM loss, joke warrant issued for NCAA Big Ten officials Share Next Stay According to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention, from Oct. 1, 2025 through Feb. 28, 2026, there were an estimated at least 26 million flu illnesses, 340,000 hospitalizations and 21,000 deaths nationwide. The season was heavily driven by influenza A (H3N2). The CDC reported that 88% of subtyped influenza A-positive specimens were H3N2 during the 2025-2026 season. This was particularly concerning because it emerged after the viruses used to formulate the 2025-2026 vaccine had already been selected. The CDC says the strain was antigenically different from the H3N2 virus included in that season’s vaccine. That kind of viral evolution can make it harder for vaccines to provide as much protection against infection. “When we have the evolution in viruses like that, it does become a little bit difficult because it’s unpredictable. So, then we can’t account for that. So the vaccinations that we have that are coming out are not as effective when we a new variant like that,” Workman said. The Food and Drug Administration updated all three components of the 2026-2027 U.S. flu vaccine. Traffic: Serious crash, vehicle fire closing part of east I-94 in Berrien County The vaccine is designed to protect against three influenza viruses. An H1N1 virus, an H3N2 virus and a B/Victoria lineage virus. Workman says the H3N2 component is particularly important heading into the new season. “So, most importantly, the component of the vaccine will be H3N2. So, being the dominant strain in the population that we suppose, that will help with protection during this season. The other two strains are typical. We have the H3 N1 strain and then we also have a B strain that we protect against as well,” Workman said. The FDA’s annual vaccine selection process is based on surveillance and analysis of influenza viruses circulating around the world and in the United States. Because influenza viruses continually change, vaccine formulations are reviewed and updated each year. Workman said one of the most important things for people to understand is that vaccination does not provide a guarantee that someone will never contract influenza. However, the goal is to reduce the risk of serious illness and complications. “So you can still get the flu with the flu vaccine. You definitely can. But oftentimes, what it’s protecting you against is from severe illness. So even though you might get flu, oftentimes with the vaccination, it protects you against hospitalizations,” Workman said. Workman said people with certain chronic medical conditions are among those he worries about most when flu activity increases. Centers for Disease Control and Prevention: Types of Seasonal Influenza Vaccines “We have to be really careful of. So those are people with type 2 diabetes and ileitis, heart failure, chronic obstructive pulmonary disease, certain kinds of cancers. Those are a more delicate population and can become sick relatively quickly,” Workman said. Young children and older adults can also face an increased risk of complications from influenza. With children returning to classrooms, Workman said influenza can move quickly through households. He said the incubation period, the time between exposure and developing an infection, can be as short as one to two days. “It can spread quickly, and it’s very virulent. That means that it spreads quickly from one person to another,” Workman said. State News: House Bill 6106 advances in committee to expand Michigan’s school mental health program He recommends that people who are sick stay home and isolate themselves when possible to reduce the risk of exposing people who are more vulnerable. For most people, influenza can cause several days of uncomfortable symptoms. But Workman said there are warning signs that should not be ignored. Those include: Shortness of breath Chest pain Severe weakness Very high fever Workman said shortness of breath is particularly concerning and should prompt someone to seek immediate medical care. Workman said another persistent misconception is that someone can catch influenza from the flu vaccine. “You cannot get the flu from the flu vaccination. It is an inactivated part of the virus and so it is not possible. Likely what happened is either you got the flu vaccination a little bit too late. It takes about two weeks for full effectiveness or it was a strain that was not covered by the vaccine,” Workman said. He also said concerns about serious or long-term effects from vaccination are among the reasons some patients choose not to get vaccinated. The CDC recommends annual flu vaccination for everyone 6 months and older, with some exceptions based on individual circumstances. Local News: Large gas leak causes all day partial closure of Westnedge in Kalamazoo Workman’s biggest concern heading into the new season isn’t necessarily the virus itself, it’s vaccination rates. “I’m most worried about the vaccination rates. It seems we’ve seen in other areas of our community where the vaccinations have rates have decreased with other types of diseases. So I have some concern that we’ll see the same thing with flu vaccination rates, which then leaves our population vulnerable,” Workman said. During the 2025-26 season, CDC estimated that by Feb. 21, about 48% of U.S. children and adolescents ages 6 months through 17 years and 47% of adults 18 and older had received a flu vaccine. Workman said getting vaccinated is not only about individual protection, but also about protecting your community. by Taboolaby Taboola SPONSORED CONTENT 2 Cards Offering $200 Bonuses After Spending Only $500CardCritics | SponsoredSponsored Undo A Billionaire's Reading List: 5 Books Cuban Says Everyone Should KnowBlinkist Magazine | SponsoredSponsored Undo Arizona: Worst Car Zip Codes for Overcharging Insurance (Is Yours On The List?)OTTO Insurance | SponsoredSponsored Undo MORE TO EXPLORE Grand Rapids men charged in connection to Kentwood homicide Undo Neighbors complain about piles of junk left behind after removal of homeless encampment Undo 'Just a discrepancy': WMU moves on from heartbreaking UofM loss with pride, grit and class Undo Trending Deadly motorcycle crash, subject of manhunt found in doghouse and more top stories Below are News Channel 3's top stories from the past week. B routine priorityPublic-health news

Don’t mistake H1N1 for routine flu; early signs matter, says doctor - The Tribune

News Brief
The Tribune article from September 2, 2026, warns about the dangers of mistaking H1N1 for a routine seasonal flu. Dr. Preeti Salhan, a Consultant in Internal Medicine at Fortis Hospital, Jalandhar, explains that H1N1 initially presents with common flu-like symptoms such as fever, body aches, and cough but can quickly escalate to pneumonia and breathing difficulties, especially in high-risk groups. The article highlights that the virus spreads through coughing, sneezing, and contact with contaminated surfaces, and that early detection and treatment are crucial. It advises that individuals with chronic conditions, pregnant women, and the elderly should be particularly cautious. The piece serves as a general health advisory, emphasizing the importance of vaccination and timely medical intervention to prevent severe complications. No specific outbreak data or case numbers are reported in the article.
Original source text (English)
H1N1 can begin like an ordinary seasonal viral infection but rapidly progress to pneumonia and breathing difficulties, especially among high-risk groups, says Dr Preeti Salhan, Consultant in Internal Medicine at Fortis Hospital, Jalandhar. Every year around this time, hospitals see the same pattern—high fever, body aches, sore throat and a cough that won’t go away. Most people assume it’s a routine seasonal viral infection, take an over-the-counter medicine and hope to get well at home. But a few days later, many are back at the hospital emergency room struggling to breathe. That second visit is often when the test comes back positive for H1N1 and by then, it’s no longer a simple flu. H1N1 (commonly known as swine flu) and formally designated as seasonal influenza A (H1N1)pdm09 by the National Centre for Disease Control behaves like a regular flu initially, but it can escalate far more rapidly. Doctors emphasise that the main danger isn’t just the virus itself, but it’s how ordinary it looks at first. Almost every patient I see with a serious case tells me the same thing. They thought it was nothing for the first two or three days. By the time patients develop breathlessness, the infection has already spread to the lungs. That initial delay often determines whether someone recovers comfortably at home or ends up needing oxygen support in a hospital bed. Why this flu is different from the others? H1N1 spreads like any other respiratory virus through cough, sneeze and touching contaminated surfaces. While a healthy adult might recover within a week like any standard viral fever, H1N1 can swiftly progress to viral pneumonia in vulnerable individuals. Pregnancy alters both lung capacity and immune function, making H1N1 a serious medical concern for expectant mothers. Chronic conditions like diabetes, heart failure, asthma, or kidney disease put extra strain on the body. An H1N1 infection can cause blood sugar levels to spike and rapidly trigger acute flares of pre-existing illnesses. Symptoms & when to seek immediate care Fever and body aches during the first 24–48 hours are common flu symptoms and generally not a cause for panic. However, seek urgent medical attention if you or a loved one experience any of the following: - Shortness of breath or abnormally rapid breathing - Persistent pain or pressure in the chest - Bluish tint on the lips or fingertips - Sudden dizziness, confusion or difficulty waking up - A sudden worsening of underlying health condition Who is most at risk? You should exercise extra caution if you fall into any of these categories: - Pregnant women (at any trimester) - Children under 5 and adults aged 65 or older - Diabetics (even those with well-managed blood sugar) - Individuals with heart, lung or kidney disease - Anyone on immunosuppressive medications or long-term steroid therapy - Healthcare workers and individuals frequently in crowded public spaces Prevention and early action can save lives Get vaccinated The annual seasonal flu shot is updated every year to target circulating H1N1 strains. It remains the most effective protection against severe illness and hospitalisation. Seek early treatment If prescribed by a doctor, antiviral drugs are most effective when started within the first 48 hours of symptom onset
View five news elements
Time
The article was published on 2026-09-02. It discusses the general behavior of H1N1 and seasonal flu, without specific event dates or time ranges.
Location and population
The article is based in Jalandhar, India, and refers to the general population, particularly high-risk groups such as pregnant women, children under 5, adults aged 65 or older, and individuals with chronic conditions like diabetes, heart failure, asthma, or kidney disease.
Event
The article highlights that H1N1 can initially resemble a routine seasonal viral infection but may rapidly progress to severe conditions like pneumonia and breathing difficulties. It is reported by Dr. Preeti Salhan, a Consultant in Internal Medicine at Fortis Hospital, Jalandhar.
Scale, impact and risk
The article does not report specific case counts, death counts, or exposure numbers. It emphasizes the risk of H1N1 progressing to severe illness, particularly in vulnerable individuals, and the importance of early treatment with antiviral drugs within the first 48 hours of symptom onset.
Response, status and uncertainty
The article discusses general prevention and response measures, including vaccination and early treatment. It does not report on active investigations, testing, or isolation efforts. The content is a general health advisory, not an outbreak report.

Doctors say they’re filling a void left by CDC on vaccines. Here’s what to know about flu, Covid-19 and RSV this fall - CNN

Doctors say they’re filling a void left by CDC on vaccines. Here’s what to know about flu, Covid-19 and RSV this fall - CNN
新闻简报The article discusses the absence of new vaccine recommendations from the CDC for the 2026-2027 flu, RSV, and Covid-19 seasons, highlighting a gap in federal guidance. In response, the American Medical Association and the University of Minnesota’s Vaccine Integrity Project have launched independent reviews, and several medical groups have released updated recommendations for respiratory virus immunizations. These recommendations include guidance on who should receive RSV vaccines, such as older adults, pregnant individuals, and infants, and emphasize the importance of vaccines in preventing serious illness and hospitalizations. The CDC’s silence is noted under the second Trump administration, and while the federal government has not responded to CNN’s request for comment, the recommendations are based on the latest evidence and public health expert insights. Vaccines are now available at major pharmacies, and insurers have indicated they will cover these immunizations. The article underscores the ongoing need for vaccine guidance despite the federal process’s breakdown.
查看新闻五要素
时间
这篇文章发表于2026-09-02。事件日期没有明确说明,但上下文提到了2026-2027年秋季和冬季,同时指出在第二任特朗普政府期间,CDC保持沉默。
地点与对象
文章讨论的是美国,关注一般人群,包括儿童、老年人、孕妇和有基础健康问题的人群。
事件
美国疾病控制与预防中心(CDC)尚未就2026-2027季节的RSV免疫接种或Covid-19 疫苗发布新的建议,导致联邦疫苗指导出现空白。美国医学协会和明尼苏达大学的疫苗诚信项目已启动独立审查以填补这一空白,多个医疗团体发布了关于呼吸道病毒免疫接种的更新建议。
规模、影响与风险
文章提到流感、SARS-CoV-2和RSV病毒将在今年秋季和冬季再次传播。它指出疫苗可在主要药店获得,保险公司也将覆盖这些疫苗接种。建议强调了疫苗在预防严重疾病、减少住院和挽救生命方面的重要性,但未报告具体病例或死亡人数。
应对、状态与不确定性
CDC对本季节的疫苗建议保持沉默,联邦政府也未对CNN的评论请求作出回应。然而,独立审查和医疗团体发布的更新建议已公布,疫苗正逐渐获得保险覆盖。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Doctors say they’re filling a void left by CDC on vaccines. Here’s what to know about flu, Covid-19 and RSV this fall - CNN

News Brief
The article discusses the absence of new vaccine recommendations from the CDC for the 2026-2027 flu, RSV, and Covid-19 seasons, highlighting a gap in federal guidance. In response, the American Medical Association and the University of Minnesota’s Vaccine Integrity Project have launched independent reviews, and several medical groups have released updated recommendations for respiratory virus immunizations. These recommendations include guidance on who should receive RSV vaccines, such as older adults, pregnant individuals, and infants, and emphasize the importance of vaccines in preventing serious illness and hospitalizations. The CDC’s silence is noted under the second Trump administration, and while the federal government has not responded to CNN’s request for comment, the recommendations are based on the latest evidence and public health expert insights. Vaccines are now available at major pharmacies, and insurers have indicated they will cover these immunizations. The article underscores the ongoing need for vaccine guidance despite the federal process’s breakdown.
Original source text (English)
Pharmacy manager Aylen Amestoy prepares to give a respiratory syncytial virus vaccine at a CVS Pharmacy in Miami in 2025. RSV, flu and Covid-19 vaccines are expected to be available at pharmacies and covered by insurance this fall and winter. Rebecca Blackwell/AP With children back in school and the looming threat of respiratory viruses like Covid-19, influenza and RSV on the horizon, many people may now be wondering how to stay healthy. Typically, the US Centers for Disease Control and Prevention launches a fall campaign around this time of year, recommending seasonal immunizations such as the flu shot and Covid-19 vaccines. But under the second Trump administration, the agency has mostly been silent this year. The CDC hasn’t issued new recommendations for RSV immunization or Covid-19 vaccines for this season. Last year, the agency updated Covid-19 vaccine guidance to shift away from a broad recommendation, instead saying that patients must consult with a healthcare provider to get the vaccine. Ad Feedback On Tuesday, the agency updated its clinical guidance for the flu shot to follow last year’s recommendations, noting on its website that “the CDC states the recommendations for seasonal influenza vaccination from the July 2025 immunization schedule remain in effect for the 2026-2027 influenza season.” The update does not mention the first mRNA flu vaccine, which was approved by the US Food and Drug Administration last month for use in older adults. To help fill the void, the American Medical Association and the University of Minnesota’s Vaccine Integrity Project launched their own independent reviews of this fall’s vaccines – including those for flu , Covid and RSV – which was published Wednesday in the medical journal JAMA. The review papers helped inform 2026-27 respiratory virus immunization recommendations released Wednesday by several medical groups, including the American Academy of Pediatrics, American Academy of Family Physicians, American College of Obstetricians and Gynecologists and the Infectious Diseases Society of America. “For more than 60 years, the United States has benefited from an established federal process for evaluating vaccine evidence and translating that science into recommendations for the American public. At the center of that process were the Centers for Disease Control and its Advisory Committee on Immunization Practices, the ACIP,” Dr. Bruce Gellin, board chair of the Vaccine Integrity Project and professor of medicine at Georgetown University, said at a news briefing. “Unfortunately today, the federal government is not fulfilling that responsibility in the way that it historically has. But the need for that work has not disappeared. Influenza, SARS-CoV-2 and the RSV viruses will circulate again this fall and winter,” Gellin said. “Clinicians will still have patients to care for. Parents will still have decisions to make for their children, and older Americans, pregnant women and people with underlying health conditions will still need to know how best to protect themselves,” he said. “We cannot allow the breakdown of a federal process to create a vacuum in evidence-based vaccine guidance.” The US Department of Health and Human Services has not responded to CNN’s request for comment on the groups’ recommendations. The recommendations provide guidance on which fall vaccines are recommended for whom and when is the optimal time to get them. Flu and RSV immunizations are now available at major pharmacies, and updated Covid-19 shots were recently approved by the FDA and are becoming available. “And really reassuringly, insurers have indicated to the major groups that they will cover these respiratory virus immunizations,” Dr. Sarah Nosal, president of the American Academy of Family Physicians, said in the news briefing. “Vaccines are one of medicine’s greatest success stories. They prevent serious illness, reduce hospitalizations and long-term complications and save lives,” Nosal said in a news release. “As federal guidance evolves, the science behind vaccines remains strong. These recommendations are grounded in the latest evidence, informed by public health experts and shaped by what family physicians are seeing every day in their patients.” RSV immunization Who should get it The groups eligible for RSV immunizations include older adults, pregnant people and infants. “People age 50 and older with high-risk conditions, and everyone over 75 years old, should get one dose,” Nosal said. “This is not an annual vaccine. If you got one before, you do not need a new one this year. Getting one should protect you throughout the entire season.” During pregnancy, RSV vaccination is recommended in the third trimester, between weeks 32 and 36. Children younger than 8 months should be immunized against RSV using a monoclonal antibody unless their mother got an RSV vaccination during pregnancy, said Dr. Andrew Racine, president of the American Academy of Pediatrics. “If the mother’s immunization status is unknown, that child should be immunized,” Racine said in the briefing. He added that children between 8 months and 19 months of age who are in certain high-risk groups for severe disease are also recommended to get the RSV monoclonal antibody. And a single dose of RSV vaccine is recommended for adults who have weakened immune systems. “Immunosuppressed individuals are at higher risk for severe disease than immunocompetent individuals,” Dr. Dan Barouch, director of the Center for Virology and Vaccine Research at Beth Israel Deaconess Medical Center, who was not involved in the new recommendations, said in an email. “The vaccines are also generally less effective in immunosuppressed individuals compared with immunocompetent individuals.” When to get it For older adults, RSV vaccinations are typically recommended in late summer and early fall. The advice slightly changed this year for pregnant people. “ACOG now recommends that pregnant individuals receive a single dose of the maternal RSV vaccine, Abrysvo, from September 1 through March 1 instead of only through January 31,” Dr. Christina Davidson, chief medical officer of the American College of Obstetricians & Gynecologists, said in the news briefing. “We made this change because the RSV disease season is now occurring later in the spring, and it is also more severe than it used to be,” Davidson said. “The extension of the eligibility window will help provide pregnant patients and their infants with additional protection.” For infants, the RSV monoclonal antibody is typically recommended around virus season, from October through March. Seasonal flu shots Who should get it Everyone 6 months and older is recommended to get an annual flu shot. “On average, flu vaccines reduce the risk of needing to go to the doctor by 30% to 60%,” Nosal said. “Adults 65 years and older should get an enhanced flu vaccine, one that is high-dose, adjuvanted or recombinant, when it’s available,” she said. “But do not delay your vaccination if that one’s not available.” And for the first time, an mRNA-based flu vaccine is available for use in adults 50 and older. The flu vaccine is also recommended during pregnancy, but pregnant people should avoid the nasal spray version, according to the recommendations. “Pregnant and postpartum people still have a significantly higher risk of developing serious complications from infection compared with non-pregnant people,” Davidson said. “The inactivated or recombinant influenza vaccines remain the only vaccines that are recommended for use in pregnancy in any trimester, so please work with your OB care clinician or pharmacist to get these,” she said. “Most importantly, if you are able to get the vaccine while you are pregnant, the immunity will also, in part, pass on to your baby.” When to get it October is the ideal time to get vaccinated, Nolan said, because “protection wanes throughout the season.” Some providers use the slogan “ flu before boo ” to remind people to get a flu shot before Halloween. Updated Covid-19 vaccines Who should get it Getting an updated Covid-19 vaccine is recommended for adults. “If you’re older than 19, we recommend getting the Covid-19 vaccine,” Nosal said. “This is especially important for people who are 65 years and older, at increased risk for severe Covid infection and those who’ve never received a Covid-19 vaccine,” she said. “For those adults 65 years and older, they should receive a second dose of the updated Covid-19 vaccine six months after that season’s initial dose to help maintain protection as immunity wanes over time.” The vaccine is also recommended for infants between 6 months and 23 months of age, and children in certain high-risk groups or those whose families request vaccination. And for people who are immunocompromised, the annual updated Covid-19 vaccine is recommended for ages 6 months and older. Last year, the CDC recommended Covid-19 vaccines based on conversations with the patient’s healthcare provider, known as shared decision-making, Barouch noted, but the agency “also emphasized that the risk-benefit of the vaccines is most favorable for individuals who are at higher risk of severe disease.” Davidson, of ACOG, said the vaccine is also recommended during pregnancy. “The most important message is this: If you are pregnant, considering pregnancy or are a new or breastfeeding parent, please get your vaccine or your annual booster. All four of the available Covid vaccines are safe for you,” Davidson said. Moderna offers two mRNA shots, Pfizer and BioNTech have another, and Sanofi and Novavax offer a more traditional protein-based version. “A recommendation for the Covid vaccine is based on more than five years of research and millions of administered doses,” Davidson said. “I want to emphasize that the evidence has found no increased risk of miscarriage, stillbirth, preterm birth, small for gestational age birth or birth defects. The risks of complications from Covid for pregnant people and their babies, however, is high. So please get the vaccine.” When to get it It’s recommended to get the updated vaccine now, but if you have been recently infected with Covid, experts advise waiting four to six months before getting the vaccine. The updated Covid-19 vaccine “targets the most recent strain that’s been circulating, and so if you want to update your protection, that’s the best way to do it,” Dr. Sandra Adamson Fryhofer, president-elect of the American Medical Association, said in the news briefing. “You know, your computer constantly needs to be updated. Well, your immune system, your immunity needs to be constantly updated too,” she said. “You’re going to get your most protection in those first few months after the vaccine. So if you have a big event – if you have to go to a wedding, or if you’re going on some fabulous trip, or you’re going to a fabulous birthday party, or just any birthday party or get-together – think about getting that vaccine a couple of weeks before that event, so you’ll stay healthy and well.” See all topics

The article was published on 2026-09-02. The event date is not explicitly stated, but the context refers to the fall and winter season of 2026-2027, with the CDC's silence noted under the second Trump administration.
Location and population
The article discusses the United States, focusing on the general population, including children, older adults, pregnant individuals, and people with underlying health conditions.
Event
The CDC has not issued new recommendations for RSV immunization or Covid-19 vaccines for the 2026-2027 season, leading to a gap in federal vaccine guidance. The American Medical Association and the University of Minnesota’s Vaccine Integrity Project have launched independent reviews to fill this void, and several medical groups have released updated recommendations for respiratory virus immunizations.
Scale, impact and risk
The article mentions that influenza, SARS-CoV-2, and RSV viruses will circulate again this fall and winter. It highlights that vaccines are available at major pharmacies and that insurers will cover these immunizations. The recommendations emphasize the importance of vaccines in preventing serious illness, reducing hospitalizations, and saving lives, but no specific case or death counts are reported.
Response, status and uncertainty
The CDC has been silent on vaccine recommendations for this season, and the federal government has not responded to CNN’s request for comment. However, independent reviews and updated recommendations from medical groups have been released, and vaccines are becoming available with insurance coverage.

Doctors release vaccine recommendations for RSV, influenza, COVID-19 - upi.com

Doctors release vaccine recommendations for RSV, influenza, COVID-19 - upi.com
新闻简报Medical organizations in the United States have released vaccine recommendations for RSV, influenza, and COVID-19 for the fall season, as the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) did not issue updated guidance this year. The recommendations, based on the latest evidence and public health experts' input, emphasize the importance of vaccines in preventing serious illness, reducing hospitalizations, and saving lives. The CDC has stated that the 2025 immunization schedule remains in effect for the 2026-2027 influenza season, and the guidelines are informed by research from the American Medical Association and the University of Minnesota's Vaccine Integrity Project. These recommendations are intended to fill a gap created by the federal government's lack of updated guidance, ensuring continued vaccine coverage for these respiratory illnesses.

Doctors release vaccine recommendations for RSV, influenza, COVID-19 - upi.com

News Brief
Medical organizations in the United States have released vaccine recommendations for RSV, influenza, and COVID-19 for the fall season, as the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) did not issue updated guidance this year. The recommendations, based on the latest evidence and public health experts' input, emphasize the importance of vaccines in preventing serious illness, reducing hospitalizations, and saving lives. The CDC has stated that the 2025 immunization schedule remains in effect for the 2026-2027 influenza season, and the guidelines are informed by research from the American Medical Association and the University of Minnesota's Vaccine Integrity Project. These recommendations are intended to fill a gap created by the federal government's lack of updated guidance, ensuring continued vaccine coverage for these respiratory illnesses.
Original source text (English)
U.S. News World News Korea Regional Review Latin America Region Odd News Entertainment Movies Music TV Sports NFL MLB NBA Soccer Voices Photos News US News World News Entertainment Sports Archive Almanac U.S. News Sept. 2, 2026 / 2:33 PM Doctors release vaccine recommendations for RSV, influenza, COVID-19 By Lisa Hornung Medical groups have released vaccine recommendations this fall in lieu of guidance from the CDC. File Photo by Javier Aparico/EPA Sept. 2 (UPI) -- A group of medical organizations has released guidance on fall vaccinations against respiratory illnesses. The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention usually launches a fall campaign to recommend seasonal vaccines for diseases like RSV, influenza and COVID-19. This year, however, the agency has not issued its updated recommendations for the respiratory virus season. In March, the American Academy of Pediatrics won a lawsuit against the federal government that blocked Health and Human Services Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. from overhauling the childhood vaccine schedule. Kennedy and President Donald Trump have said that children get too many vaccines and have reduced the number of vaccines routinely recommended. They have also linked vaccines to autism, which has been proven untrue by research. In response, the American Academy of Pediatrics, the American College of Obstetricians and Gynecologists, the American Academy of Family Physicians, the Infectious Diseases Society of America each issued guidelines for fall immunizations. They based those guidelines on research from the American Medical Association and the University of Minnesota's Vaccine Integrity Project Last year, the CDC changed the COVID-19 recommendations to say that patients should talk to their own doctors. On Tuesday, the agency said, "the CDC states the recommendations for seasonal influenza vaccination from the July 2025 immunization schedule remain in effect for the 2026-2027 influenza season." It doesn't mention the new mRNA flu vaccine, approved by the U.S. Food and Drug Administration last month. "For more than 60 years, the United States has benefited from an established federal process for evaluating vaccine evidence and translating that science into recommendations for the American public. At the center of that process were the Centers for Disease Control and its Advisory Committee on Immunization Practices, the ACIP," CNN reported Dr. Bruce Gellin, board chair of the Vaccine Integrity Project and professor of medicine at Georgetown University, said at a press briefing. "Unfortunately today, the federal government is not fulfilling that responsibility in the way that it historically has. But the need for that work has not disappeared. Influenza, SARS-CoV-2 [COVID-19] and the RSV viruses will circulate again this fall and winter," Gellin said. "We cannot allow the breakdown of a federal process to create a vacuum in evidence-based vaccine guidance," he added. Dr. Sarah Nosal, president of the American Academy of Family Physicians, said in the news briefing that insurance companies have indicated they will cover the vaccines. "Vaccines are one of medicine's greatest success stories. They prevent serious illness, reduce hospitalizations and long-term complications and save lives," Nosal said in a press release. "As federal guidance evolves, the science behind vaccines remains strong. These recommendations are grounded in the latest evidence, informed by public health experts and shaped by what family physicians are seeing every day in their patients." B routine priorityPublic-health news

Singapore influenza cases climb as positivity rate hits 36.6pc, health agency monitors situation - Malay Mail

News Brief
Singapore has seen an increase in influenza cases, with the positivity rate rising to 36.6% in the week from August 16 to 22, 2026, compared to 24.0% in the previous week. This increase was reported by the Communicable Diseases Agency (CDA), which emphasized that there is no indication of heightened severity. The CDA noted that current positivity levels are still below the historical peak of 43.9% recorded in January 2025. While no specific case or death numbers were provided, the agency is actively monitoring the situation and has advised high-risk individuals and those with underlying medical conditions to get their annual influenza vaccinations. The report was first published on September 2, 2026, and highlights the seasonal nature of influenza in Singapore, with activity typically peaking between May and August and December and March. The CDA also mentioned that factors such as international travel and population mixing can influence the timing of these peaks.
Original source text (English)

First Published: Wednesday, 02 Sep 2026 9:25 AM MYT × Subscribe to Newsletter Subscribe SINGAPORE, Sept 2 — Singapore recorded an increase in influenza cases, with the positivity rate rising to 36.6 per cent in the week from Aug 16 to 22, 2026, compared with 24.0 per cent in the previous week. The Communicable Diseases Agency (CDA), however, said there was no indication that the infections were more severe than usual. “The current influenza positivity levels remain below last year’s peak of 43.9 per cent observed in January 2025. CDA is monitoring the situation,” it said in a statement on Tuesday. According to the CDA, Singapore national surveillance data indicate that seasonal influenza viruses circulate throughout the year, with periods of higher activity typically observed between May and August and December and March. These bimodal peaks broadly coincide with the Northern and Southern Hemisphere winter periods, but the timing may vary from year to year due to factors such as international travel and population mixing patterns, it added. The agency also advised individuals at higher risk of developing severe influenza infections, and vulnerable groups with underlying medical conditions, to receive their influenza vaccinations at least once a year. — Bernama FURTHER QUESTIONS? The positivity rate for influenza in Singapore increased to 36.6% from August 16 to 22, 2026, compared to 24.0% in the previous week. The report was first published on September 2, 2026.
Location and population
Singapore, with the focus on the general population and individuals at higher risk of severe influenza infections.
Event
Singapore recorded an increase in influenza cases, with the positivity rate rising to 36.6% in the week from August 16 to 22, 2026, according to the Communicable Diseases Agency (CDA). The CDA stated there was no indication of increased severity despite the rise in cases.
Scale, impact and risk
The influenza positivity rate reached 36.6% in the week from August 16 to 22, 2026, compared to 24.0% the previous week. The CDA noted that current positivity levels remain below the historical peak of 43.9% observed in January 2025. No specific case or death counts were reported, but the agency emphasized monitoring the situation.
Response, status and uncertainty
The CDA is monitoring the situation and has stated there is no indication of increased severity. The agency also advised individuals at higher risk to receive annual influenza vaccinations.

B routine priorityPublic-health news

News article (Chinese original; verified English title unavailable)

Original title (Chinese)
流感流行提前?第35周数据给出信号 - thepaper.cn
News Brief
Influenza activity in China showed early season signals during week 35 (August 24‐30, 2026), with nucleic acid positivity rising to 17.7%, up from 2.4% the previous year, according to the China CDC and National Influenza Center. Southern provinces recorded a positivity rate of 20.8%, driven by H3N2, while national rates crossed the 17% threshold, suggesting a possible early influenza season. No specific case or death counts were reported, and the article did not assign a quantified risk level. In response, health authorities advised all individuals aged six months and older without contraindications to receive the influenza vaccine promptly, emphasizing that vaccination should not be delayed. Uncertainty persists regarding the true timing of the season, as post‐school opening trends in the coming weeks will clarify whether the observed rise reflects an actual early onset or merely pre‐season fluctuations.

澎湃号·湃客 > 流感流行提前?第35周数据给出信号 儿科严医生 复旦大学儿科学博士 2026-09-05 18:06 上海 2026 年 9 月 3 日,中国疾控中心发布了第 35 周(8 月 24 日 至 8 月 30 日)全国急性呼吸道传染病哨点监测数据。同日,国家流感中心也发布了《流感监测周报》。 第 35 周最显著的变化是:新冠病毒核酸阳性率大幅下降(- 3.7%),而流感病毒核酸阳性率继续攀升(+ 1.3%)。 两者在第 35 周完成交叉,流感跃居首位(17.7%)。 第 35 周《流感监测周报》显示: 第 35 周,南方省份流感病毒核酸阳性率已经达到 20.8%,以甲流H3N2驱动。而第 34 周是 18.3%。 至于其他常见呼吸道病原体,目前主要还是小幅波动,还没有看到明显的流行态势。 第 35 周数据反映的还是开学前的情况。 随着学校和幼儿园陆续开学,部分常见呼吸道病原体的传播应该会加快。 2026年第 35 周,流感核酸阳性率17.7%,而去年同期(2025年第 35 周),流感核酸阳性率仅2.4%。看上去,2026年的流感季像是“提前了”。 但更合理的解释也许是:南方夏季流感还没有完全退下去,B(Victoria)之后,H3N2 亚分支又开始接力上升;而既往,中国南方部分地区就存在H3N2夏季流行的特点。 几股力量叠加,使得今年8月的流感阳性率明显高于去年同期。 至于这是否意味着今年全国范围内秋冬流感季真正提前了,目前无法给出确切结论,要看开学后3~4周南北方的走势。 不论如何,对于南方地区的适龄人群,我建议:流感疫苗应尽快接种,不必观望。 参考来源 https://www.chinacdc.cn/jksj/jksj04_14275/202609/t20260903_1839779.html https://www.chinacdc.cn/jksj/jksj04_14249/202609/t20260903_1839747.html https://journals.plos.org/plospathogens/article?id=10.1371%2Fjournal.ppat.1011046 https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9501233/ 原标题:《流感流行提前?第35周数据给出信号》 阅读原文 特别声明 本文为澎湃号作者或机构在澎湃新闻上传并发布,仅代表该作者或机构观点,不代表澎湃新闻的观点或立场,澎湃新闻仅提供信息发布平台。申请澎湃号请用电脑访问https://renzheng.thepaper.cn。 14 相关推荐 尿液出现这种颜色,可能是癌症信号 肿瘤专科医生 14小时前 别等确诊糖尿病才后悔!血糖超标的信号,年轻人也要警惕 腾讯医典 23小时前 儿童心理咨询:脾气暴躁、易怒,可能是孩子发出的求助信号 听说吧心理咨询 15小时前 中疾控:建议所有6月龄及以上且无接种禁忌的人接种流感疫苗 绿政公署 4天前 24小时最热 00:34 驳船往长江倾倒黑色固体“十秒清空一艘船”?武汉市生态环境局:已赴现场调查 锋线视频 20小时前 458评 郭德纲歪曲篡改抗战歌曲,武汉文旅局发布处理通报 直击现场 12小时前 216评 星宇股份发布近期调岗减员错误行为的相关情况及整改措施 10%公司 17小时前 398评 起售价过万!小米18 Fold发布,雷军谈造芯:只要开始追赶,小米就走在赢的路上 10%公司 9小时前 37评 央媒调查一企业私设码头:多头监管4年关不掉,现场执法被暴力阻挠 直击现场 22小时前 97评 你有权知道更多 下载澎湃新闻客户端 下载APP 脱离遥控器,人形机器人全自主格斗!宇树科技又公布新进展 马上评|冒充公职人员挂账消费何以能得逞? 2026-03-13 17:01 阅读量:1.4万+ 华人号:华人头条-阿根廷 华人头条阿根廷3月13日讯/Cl:阿根廷2026年流感疫苗接种活动将于本周三在全国启动。与去年相比,今年的接种计划提前了三周,目的是在呼吸道病毒高发季节到来之前,为重点人群建立免疫保护。同时,目前正在传播的一种新的甲型流感A(H3N2)变异株具有更高的传播性。 阿根廷卫生部(Ministerio de Salud)表示,此次接种活动的目标是为可能出现并发症或重症的群体提供更强的免疫保护,并减轻流感对医疗系统的压力。 这一决定与近年来呼吸道病毒季节提前有关,同时也与新的甲型流感A(H3N2)变异株传播有关,该病毒具有更强的传染能力。 通过提前规划和物流安排,国家卫生部门共采购了8,160,000剂疫苗,其中包括: 4,700,000剂成人流感疫苗 2,300,000剂佐剂型流感疫苗(aTIV) 1,160,000剂儿童流感疫苗 疫苗从2月初开始分发至各省。目前已向全国各地发放超过180万剂,预计在3月底前还将追加发放1,018,640剂。 与此同时,阿根廷卫生部(Ministerio de Salud)还制定了一项综合准备策略,包括对全国医疗团队进行培训,并发布《2026年流感疫苗接种技术指南》(Lineamientos Técnicos de Vacunación Antigripal 2026)。该文件详细说明了接种目标人群和优先群体、疫苗成分、国家免疫计划中的接种方案以及具体实施标准,以确保全国范围内统一、规范地开展接种工作。 2026年流感疫苗接种对象 建议接种流感疫苗的人群包括: 6个月至24个月的儿童 医务人员 65岁及以上老年人 孕妇 产妇(若在怀孕期间未接种,可在出院后10天内接种) 任何年龄但具有基础疾病或风险因素的人群 疫苗可在全国各地的公立医院、卫生中心和疫苗接种点免费接种,并且可以与国家免疫计划(Calendario Nacional)中的其他疫苗同时接种。 卫生部门还呼吁公众积极接种,并强调:“国家免疫计划中的所有疫苗都是安全的,经过多年验证,有科学证据支持,并且能够有效预防疾病。” (完)
